# 98. EA-IMETA Master Architecture Governance Framework

## 98.1 Purpose

The EA-IMETA Master Architecture Governance Framework defines how the complete EA-IMETA architecture shall be directed, controlled, maintained, reviewed, approved, implemented, measured, and evolved.

Governance ensures that EA-IMETA remains:

- Coherent
- Authoritative
- Consistent
- Traceable
- Secure
- Controlled
- Adaptable
- Value-oriented

Governance shall therefore provide the mechanisms required to prevent architectural fragmentation while allowing appropriate decentralization and specialist autonomy.

---

# 99. Governance Principle

## 99.1 Fundamental Principle

EA-IMETA governance shall follow:

CLEAR AUTHORITY

+

DEFINED ACCOUNTABILITY

+

DELEGATED RESPONSIBILITY

+

CONTROLLED DECISION-MAKING

+

TRANSPARENT EVIDENCE

+

CONTINUOUS ASSURANCE

Governance shall not exist primarily to slow architecture decisions.

Its purpose is to ensure that architecture decisions are:

UNDERSTOOD

AUTHORIZED

TRACEABLE

PROPORTIONATE

REVERSIBLE WHERE POSSIBLE

AND ALIGNED WITH ENTERPRISE VALUE

---

# 100. Governance Objectives

The governance framework shall achieve the following objectives:

1. Maintain architectural coherence.

2. Protect strategic alignment.

3. Control architecture risk.

4. Maintain architectural standards.

5. Protect enterprise value.

6. Ensure accountability.

7. Govern architectural change.

8. Maintain traceability.

9. Control exceptions.

10. Support continuous architecture evolution.

---

# 101. Governance Scope

EA-IMETA governance shall apply across:

Strategy

Business Architecture

Operating Model

Capabilities

Organization

Processes

Information

Data

Applications

Technology

Cloud

AI

Agents

Security

Governance

Risk

Compliance

Resilience

Engineering

Operations

Architecture Intelligence

Digital Twin

Value

Investment

Sustainability

Autonomous Enterprise

Governance shall therefore be cross-domain.

---

# 102. Governance Domains

The governance system shall consist of several complementary governance dimensions.

Architecture Governance

Data Governance

AI Governance

Agent Governance

Security Governance

Technology Governance

Risk Governance

Compliance Governance

Investment Governance

Change Governance

Information Governance

Operational Governance

These governance dimensions shall operate within the common Master Architecture governance model.

---

# 103. Governance Hierarchy

## 103.1 Governance Structure

The governance hierarchy shall be:

ENTERPRISE GOVERNANCE
        |
        v
EA-IMETA MASTER GOVERNANCE
        |
        +-----------------------------+
        |                             |
        v                             v
DOMAIN GOVERNANCE               CROSS-CUTTING GOVERNANCE
        |                             |
        +--------------+--------------+
                       |
                       v
              SOLUTION GOVERNANCE
                       |
                       v
             IMPLEMENTATION GOVERNANCE
                       |
                       v
              OPERATIONAL GOVERNANCE

---

## 103.2 Enterprise Governance

Enterprise Governance establishes the overall:

Purpose

Strategy

Risk Appetite

Investment Direction

Accountability

Regulatory Responsibility

---

## 103.3 Master Architecture Governance

Master Architecture Governance establishes:

Architecture Principles

Meta-Model

Canonical Vocabulary

Architecture Standards

Reference Structures

Architecture Authority

Architecture Decision Framework

---

## 103.4 Domain Governance

Domain Governance manages specific architectural domains.

Examples include:

Data Architecture Governance

AI Architecture Governance

Security Architecture Governance

Technology Architecture Governance

---

## 103.5 Solution Governance

Solution Governance ensures that individual solutions conform to relevant architecture principles, requirements, standards, and approved patterns.

---

## 103.6 Operational Governance

Operational Governance ensures that implemented architecture continues to operate within approved:

Performance

Security

Risk

Compliance

Availability

Cost

Service

Architecture

---

# 104. Governance Authorities

## 104.1 Architecture Authority

EA-IMETA shall define an Architecture Authority responsible for maintaining architectural integrity.

The Architecture Authority shall be responsible for:

Architecture Principles

Architecture Standards

Architecture Decisions

Architecture Exceptions

Architecture Conformance

Architecture Evolution

---

## 104.2 Domain Architecture Authority

Each major architecture domain should have an appropriate domain authority.

Examples:

Chief Enterprise Architect

Domain Architect

Data Architect

Security Architect

AI Architect

Technology Architect

Solution Architect

The exact organizational titles may vary.

The responsibilities shall remain clear even when titles differ.

---

# 105. Architecture Accountability

## 105.1 Accountability Principle

Every material architecture decision shall have an accountable authority.

The model shall be:

DECISION
    |
    +-- has
          |
          v
ACCOUNTABLE AUTHORITY

---

## 105.2 Accountability Requirements

The accountable authority shall have:

Defined Scope

Defined Authority

Defined Responsibility

Defined Escalation Path

Defined Decision Rights

---

# 106. Delegated Authority

## 106.1 Principle

Not every architecture decision requires central approval.

Authority shall be delegated according to:

Risk

Impact

Materiality

Complexity

Reversibility

Strategic Importance

Regulatory Impact

---

## 106.2 Decision Delegation

The delegation model shall be:

LOW IMPACT
    |
    v
LOCAL DECISION

MEDIUM IMPACT
    |
    v
DOMAIN DECISION

HIGH IMPACT
    |
    v
MASTER ARCHITECTURE DECISION

ENTERPRISE-CRITICAL
    |
    v
EXECUTIVE / ENTERPRISE AUTHORITY

---

# 107. Architecture Decision Rights

## 107.1 Purpose

Decision rights define who may make specific types of architecture decisions.

Examples include:

Technology Selection

Architecture Pattern Selection

Data Ownership

Security Control Selection

AI Model Approval

Agent Authority

Cloud Strategy

Integration Standards

Architecture Exceptions

---

## 107.2 Decision Rights Matrix

A future EA-IMETA implementation should maintain a decision rights matrix containing:

Decision Type

Decision Scope

Decision Owner

Required Consultation

Approval Authority

Escalation Authority

Evidence Requirement

---

# 108. Architecture Review

## 108.1 Purpose

Architecture Review provides structured evaluation before significant architecture decisions are implemented.

Reviews shall evaluate:

Strategic Alignment

Business Fit

Technical Fit

Security

Risk

Compliance

Data

AI

Operations

Value

Cost

Resilience

Future Evolution

---

## 108.2 Review Types

EA-IMETA shall support:

Concept Review

Architecture Review

Design Review

Security Review

Data Review

AI Review

Agent Review

Solution Review

Implementation Review

Post-Implementation Review

---

# 109. Architecture Review Lifecycle

The review lifecycle shall be:

SUBMISSION

↓

INITIAL SCREENING

↓

ARCHITECTURE ANALYSIS

↓

DOMAIN REVIEW

↓

RISK AND SECURITY REVIEW

↓

DECISION

↓

APPROVAL

↓

IMPLEMENTATION

↓

CONFORMANCE REVIEW

---

# 110. Architecture Decision Record

## 110.1 Purpose

Material architecture decisions shall be documented using an Architecture Decision Record.

The minimum structure shall include:

Decision ID

Title

Context

Problem

Drivers

Options

Evaluation Criteria

Decision

Rationale

Evidence

Risks

Consequences

Dependencies

Authority

Date

Review Date

Status

---

## 110.2 Decision Status

Architecture decisions may have:

Proposed

Under Review

Approved

Rejected

Superseded

Deprecated

---

# 111. Architecture Exception Management

## 111.1 Purpose

Exceptions are permitted where strict compliance with an architecture standard would create disproportionate cost, risk, or operational difficulty.

Exceptions shall be controlled.

---

## 111.2 Exception Requirements

An exception shall identify:

Exception ID

Requestor

Affected Architecture

Requirement Being Excepted

Reason

Business Justification

Risk

Compensating Controls

Duration

Owner

Approval Authority

Review Date

---

## 111.3 Exception Principle

An exception shall be:

EXPLICIT

JUSTIFIED

TIME-BOUND

OWNED

REVIEWED

Exceptions shall not become permanent undocumented architecture.

---

# 112. Architecture Conformance

## 112.1 Purpose

Architecture Conformance determines whether an implementation follows approved architecture.

Conformance shall evaluate:

Principles

Standards

Patterns

Requirements

Security Controls

Governance Controls

Architecture Decisions

---

## 112.2 Conformance Outcomes

Possible outcomes include:

Conformant

Conformant with Conditions

Non-Conformant

Exception Required

Not Applicable

---

# 113. Architecture Assurance

Architecture Assurance provides independent or semi-independent confirmation that governance requirements are being followed.

Assurance may include:

Architecture Audits

Security Assessments

Control Testing

Architecture Reviews

Operational Reviews

Risk Assessments

Compliance Reviews

Value Assessments

---

# 114. Governance and Risk

## 114.1 Risk-Based Governance

Governance intensity shall be proportional to risk.

LOW RISK
    |
    v
LIGHT GOVERNANCE

MEDIUM RISK
    |
    v
STANDARD GOVERNANCE

HIGH RISK
    |
    v
ENHANCED GOVERNANCE

CRITICAL RISK
    |
    v
EXECUTIVE GOVERNANCE

---

## 114.2 Architecture Risk Categories

Architecture risk shall include:

Strategic Risk

Business Risk

Technology Risk

Security Risk

Data Risk

AI Risk

Agent Risk

Operational Risk

Financial Risk

Compliance Risk

Resilience Risk

Vendor Risk

Architecture Debt

---

# 115. AI Governance

## 115.1 AI Governance Principle

AI shall be governed throughout its lifecycle.

AI IDEA

↓

AI DESIGN

↓

AI DEVELOPMENT

↓

AI VALIDATION

↓

AI APPROVAL

↓

AI DEPLOYMENT

↓

AI MONITORING

↓

AI REVIEW

↓

AI RETIREMENT

---

## 115.2 AI Governance Requirements

AI systems shall have defined:

Purpose

Owner

Model

Data

Risk Classification

Security Requirements

Evaluation Criteria

Human Oversight

Monitoring

Change Controls

Retirement Conditions

---

# 116. Agent Governance

## 116.1 Agent Governance Principle

Agents require explicit governance because they may perform actions rather than merely provide information.

Every governed agent shall have:

Identity

Purpose

Objective

Authority

Permissions

Tools

Data Access

Policies

Limits

Monitoring

Auditability

Human Escalation

---

## 116.2 Agent Autonomy Levels

EA-IMETA shall support graduated autonomy:

LEVEL 0
No Autonomy

LEVEL 1
Assistance

LEVEL 2
Recommendation

LEVEL 3
Supervised Action

LEVEL 4
Conditional Autonomy

LEVEL 5
High Autonomy

Higher autonomy shall require stronger controls.

---

# 117. Human Accountability

Autonomous systems shall not eliminate organizational accountability.

The principle shall be:

AUTOMATION MAY DELEGATE ACTION

BUT

ACCOUNTABILITY MUST REMAIN DEFINED

---

## 117.1 Human Oversight

Human oversight shall be required where appropriate based on:

Risk

Impact

Irreversibility

Legal Requirements

Ethical Considerations

Customer Impact

Safety

Financial Impact

---

# 118. Architecture Change Governance

## 118.1 Change Principle

Architecture change shall be controlled according to impact.

CHANGE REQUEST

↓

IMPACT ANALYSIS

↓

RISK ASSESSMENT

↓

ARCHITECTURE REVIEW

↓

DECISION

↓

IMPLEMENTATION

↓

VALIDATION

↓

ARCHITECTURE UPDATE

---

## 118.2 Change Categories

Changes may be classified as:

Minor

Significant

Major

Strategic

Emergency

---

# 119. Emergency Architecture Change

Emergency changes may bypass normal governance where necessary to:

Protect People

Protect Security

Restore Critical Services

Prevent Major Loss

Meet Regulatory Requirements

Emergency decisions shall subsequently undergo retrospective review.

---

# 120. Governance Evidence

Governance decisions shall be evidence-based.

Evidence may include:

Architecture Models

Financial Analysis

Security Analysis

Risk Analysis

Operational Data

Testing

Simulation

Performance Metrics

Stakeholder Evidence

AI Evaluation

Historical Decisions

---

# 121. Governance Transparency

Governance shall provide sufficient transparency to allow stakeholders to understand:

What Was Decided

Why It Was Decided

Who Decided

What Evidence Was Used

What Risks Were Accepted

What Alternatives Were Rejected

When It Will Be Revisited

---

# 122. Architecture Governance Repository

All significant governance artifacts shall be maintained in an Architecture Governance Repository.

The repository shall contain:

Architecture Decisions

Architecture Principles

Standards

Policies

Exceptions

Reviews

Conformance Assessments

Risk Assessments

Architecture Requirements

Approval Records

---

# 123. Governance Metrics

Governance effectiveness shall be measured.

Initial metrics shall include:

Architecture Decision Cycle Time

Architecture Review Cycle Time

Conformance Rate

Exception Rate

Exception Age

Architecture Debt

Decision Reuse

Standards Adoption

Architecture Risk Exposure

Governance Cost

Post-Implementation Defects

---

# 124. Governance Performance

Governance shall be assessed not only by control but also by efficiency.

The objective shall be:

MAXIMUM ARCHITECTURAL CONTROL

WITH

MINIMUM UNNECESSARY FRICTION

---

# 125. Governance Maturity

EA-IMETA governance maturity shall progress through:

LEVEL 1
Ad Hoc

LEVEL 2
Documented

LEVEL 3
Controlled

LEVEL 4
Integrated

LEVEL 5
Adaptive

LEVEL 6
Intelligent

---

## 125.1 Level 1 – Ad Hoc

Architecture decisions are primarily informal and reactive.

---

## 125.2 Level 2 – Documented

Basic governance structures and procedures exist.

---

## 125.3 Level 3 – Controlled

Architecture decisions, standards, reviews, and exceptions are formally managed.

---

## 125.4 Level 4 – Integrated

Architecture governance is integrated with:

Strategy

Investment

Risk

Security

Transformation

Operations

---

## 125.5 Level 5 – Adaptive

Governance adapts dynamically according to:

Risk

Context

Architecture Change

Business Conditions

---

## 125.6 Level 6 – Intelligent

Architecture intelligence, AI, simulation, knowledge graphs, and digital twins support governance decisions.

---

# 126. Governance Automation

Governance processes may be automated where appropriate.

Potential automation includes:

Policy Validation

Architecture Conformance

Security Checks

Dependency Analysis

Risk Detection

Standards Validation

Document Classification

Impact Analysis

Architecture Drift Detection

Automation shall remain subject to governance.

---

# 127. Architecture Drift

## 127.1 Definition

Architecture Drift is the gradual divergence between approved architecture and actual implemented or operational architecture.

---

## 127.2 Drift Detection

Drift may be identified through:

Configuration Data

Operational Telemetry

Architecture Scans

Dependency Analysis

Security Monitoring

Application Discovery

Cloud Inventory

Data Discovery

---

## 127.3 Drift Response

DETECT

↓

ASSESS

↓

CLASSIFY

↓

CORRECT

OR

APPROVE AS NEW STATE

↓

UPDATE ARCHITECTURE

---

# 128. Governance Feedback Loop

Governance shall operate as a continuous feedback mechanism:

DECISION

↓

IMPLEMENTATION

↓

OBSERVATION

↓

MEASUREMENT

↓

ASSURANCE

↓

LEARNING

↓

GOVERNANCE UPDATE

This prevents governance from becoming a static administrative process.

---

# 129. Governance Integration with Transformation

Architecture governance shall be integrated with transformation governance.

STRATEGY

↓

TRANSFORMATION PORTFOLIO

↓

INITIATIVES

↓

ARCHITECTURE

↓

IMPLEMENTATION

↓

OUTCOMES

↓

VALUE

Governance shall ensure that transformation investment remains aligned with architecture and enterprise objectives.

---

# 130. Governance Integration with Investment

Architecture decisions affecting material investments shall be linked to portfolio governance.

INVESTMENT

↓

INITIATIVE

↓

ARCHITECTURE DECISION

↓

IMPLEMENTATION

↓

BENEFIT

↓

VALUE REALIZATION

---

# 131. Governance Integration with Operations

Operational evidence shall feed architecture governance.

Examples include:

Performance

Incidents

Security Events

Capacity

Cost

Availability

Customer Experience

Technical Debt

Architecture Drift

This creates a continuous connection between architecture and reality.

---

# 132. Governance Operating Model

The complete governance operating model shall be:

                         ENTERPRISE PURPOSE
                                  |
                                  v
                              STRATEGY
                                  |
                                  v
                       MASTER ARCHITECTURE
                                  |
                                  v
                             GOVERNANCE
                                  |
             +--------------------+--------------------+
             |                    |                    |
             v                    v                    v
        PRINCIPLES            STANDARDS             POLICIES
             |                    |                    |
             +--------------------+--------------------+
                                  |
                                  v
                        ARCHITECTURE DECISIONS
                                  |
                                  v
                            IMPLEMENTATION
                                  |
                                  v
                              OPERATIONS
                                  |
                                  v
                             ASSURANCE
                                  |
                                  v
                              LEARNING
                                  |
                                  v
                              EVOLUTION

---

# 133. Section 6 Summary

EA-IMETA-MASTER-01 Section 6 establishes the governance framework for the complete EA-IMETA ecosystem.

The fundamental governance principle is:

GOVERNANCE MUST PROVIDE CONTROL
WITHOUT CREATING UNNECESSARY FRICTION.

The governance model establishes:

Authority

Accountability

Decision Rights

Architecture Review

Architecture Decisions

Exception Management

Conformance

Assurance

Risk Governance

AI Governance

Agent Governance

Change Governance

Architecture Drift Management

Governance Metrics

Governance Maturity

Governance Automation

The resulting governance model ensures that EA-IMETA remains:

COHERENT

CONTROLLED

ACCOUNTABLE

TRACEABLE

RISK-AWARE

VALUE-ORIENTED

ADAPTIVE

Governance therefore becomes a continuous architectural capability rather than a one-time approval process.

---

# 134. EA-IMETA Master Architecture Traceability Framework

## 134.1 Purpose

The EA-IMETA Traceability Framework defines how architectural relationships shall be established, maintained, verified, and used throughout the complete EA-IMETA ecosystem.

Traceability is essential because EA-IMETA is intended to function as a connected architecture system rather than as a collection of independent documents.

The Traceability Framework shall enable the enterprise to answer:

WHY DOES THIS EXIST?

WHAT DOES THIS SUPPORT?

WHAT DEPENDS ON IT?

WHAT WILL BE AFFECTED IF IT CHANGES?

WHO IS ACCOUNTABLE?

WHAT EVIDENCE SUPPORTS IT?

WHAT VALUE DOES IT CREATE?

---

# 135. Traceability Principle

## 135.1 Fundamental Principle

EA-IMETA shall follow:

EVERY MATERIAL ARCHITECTURAL ELEMENT
SHALL BE TRACEABLE TO ITS CONTEXT,
PURPOSE, OWNER, RELATIONSHIPS,
DECISIONS, IMPLEMENTATION AND OUTCOME.

Traceability shall therefore be treated as a fundamental architecture capability.

---

## 135.2 Traceability Chain

The canonical traceability chain shall be:

PURPOSE
   |
   v
STRATEGY
   |
   v
OBJECTIVE
   |
   v
OUTCOME
   |
   v
CAPABILITY
   |
   v
REQUIREMENT
   |
   v
ARCHITECTURE
   |
   v
IMPLEMENTATION
   |
   v
OPERATION
   |
   v
MEASUREMENT
   |
   v
VALUE

---

# 136. Traceability Dimensions

Traceability shall operate across several dimensions.

Strategic Traceability

Business Traceability

Capability Traceability

Requirement Traceability

Architecture Traceability

Technology Traceability

Data Traceability

AI Traceability

Agent Traceability

Security Traceability

Risk Traceability

Investment Traceability

Implementation Traceability

Operational Traceability

Value Traceability

---

# 137. Strategic Traceability

## 137.1 Purpose

Strategic Traceability connects architecture to enterprise strategy.

The relationship shall be:

Enterprise Purpose
        |
        v
Strategic Direction
        |
        v
Strategic Objective
        |
        v
Capability
        |
        v
Architecture

---

## 137.2 Strategic Alignment Test

Every material architecture initiative should be capable of answering:

Which strategic objective does this support?

If no meaningful relationship exists, the initiative should be reassessed.

---

Business Traceability connects architecture to business operations.

Business Objective
        |
        v
Capability
        |
        v

        |
        v
Process
        |
        v
Business Service

This allows architecture teams to determine how technology and other architectural elements support actual business outcomes.

---

Data

AI

Agents

Investment

Risk

# 153. Bidirectional Traceability

EA-IMETA shall support both forward and backward traceability.

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓
OUTCOME

   ↓

   ↓

Affected AI

Affected Agents

Affected Security Controls

Affected Risks

Affected Outcomes

# 156. Traceability Matrix

A future EA-IMETA implementation should maintain traceability matrices.

Example:

| Source | Relationship | Target | Evidence | Owner | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Strategy | drives | Capability | Strategy Document | Strategy Owner | Active |
| Capability | realized by | Process | Capability Model | Domain Owner | Active |
| Process | supported by | Application | Process Model | Application Owner | Active |
| Application | implemented on | Technology | CMDB | Technology Owner | Active |
| Risk | mitigated by | Control | Risk Assessment | Risk Owner | Active |

---

# 157. Traceability Quality

Traceability shall be evaluated according to:

Completeness

Accuracy

Currency

Consistency

Evidence

Ownership

Bidirectionality

Granularity

Automation

---

# 158. Traceability Coverage

A traceability coverage metric may be established:

TRACEABILITY COVERAGE

=

TRACEABLE MATERIAL ELEMENTS

/

TOTAL MATERIAL ELEMENTS

The metric may be expressed as a percentage.

---

# 159. Traceability Debt

## 159.1 Definition

Traceability Debt is the accumulated absence, weakness, or obsolescence of required architecture relationships and evidence.

Traceability Debt may create:

Unknown Dependencies

Hidden Risk

Poor Impact Analysis

Duplicate Work

Governance Weakness

Architecture Drift

Decision Uncertainty

---

# 160. Traceability Automation

Traceability should increasingly be automated.

Potential mechanisms include:

Architecture Repository

Knowledge Graph

CMDB

Data Catalog

Application Discovery

Cloud Inventory

Source Code Analysis

Dependency Mapping

AI-Assisted Document Analysis

Digital Twin

---

# 161. Traceability and AI

AI may assist in identifying missing relationships.

For example:

DOCUMENT

↓

AI ANALYSIS

↓

POTENTIAL CONCEPT

↓

POTENTIAL RELATIONSHIP

↓

HUMAN VALIDATION

↓

CANONICAL RELATIONSHIP

AI shall not automatically establish authoritative architecture relationships without appropriate governance.

---

# 162. Traceability and Knowledge Graph

The Knowledge Graph shall provide the structural foundation for machine-readable traceability.

Conceptually:

NODE
  |
  +-- RELATIONSHIP
          |
          v
       NODE

Each relationship should be capable of carrying:

Relationship Type

Source

Target

Evidence

Confidence

Owner

Validity

Timestamp

---

# 163. Traceability and Digital Twin

The Digital Twin shall extend traceability into operational reality.

ARCHITECTURE MODEL
       |
       v
DIGITAL TWIN
       |
       v
OBSERVED STATE
       |
       v
OPERATIONAL EVENT
       |
       v
ARCHITECTURE IMPACT

This enables continuous comparison between designed and observed architecture.

---

# 164. Traceability and Architecture Intelligence

Architecture Intelligence shall use traceability to answer:

WHAT CHANGED?

WHY DID IT CHANGE?

WHAT DOES IT AFFECT?

WHAT DEPENDS ON IT?

WHAT RISK DOES IT CREATE?

WHAT VALUE DOES IT ENABLE?

WHAT SHOULD WE DO NEXT?

---

# 165. Traceability Governance

Traceability shall itself be governed.

The governance model shall define:

Required Relationships

Required Evidence

Ownership

Validation Rules

Quality Thresholds

Review Frequency

Retention

Access

Change Management

---

# 166. Traceability Lifecycle

The lifecycle shall be:

CREATE

↓

VALIDATE

↓

CONNECT

↓

VERIFY

↓

USE

↓

MONITOR

↓

UPDATE

↓

RETIRE

---

# 167. Traceability Operating Model

The complete operating model shall be:

                        MASTER ARCHITECTURE
                                 |
                                 v
                            META-MODEL
                                 |
                                 v
                         TRACEABILITY MODEL
                                 |
             +-------------------+-------------------+
             |                   |                   |
             v                   v                   v
        DOCUMENTS            ENTITIES          RELATIONSHIPS
             |                   |                   |
             +-------------------+-------------------+
                                 |
                                 v
                         KNOWLEDGE GRAPH
                                 |
                                 v
                         IMPACT ANALYSIS
                                 |
                                 v
                      ARCHITECTURE INTELLIGENCE
                                 |
                                 v
                           DECISION SUPPORT

---

# 168. Section 7 Summary

EA-IMETA-MASTER-01 Section 7 establishes the Traceability Framework.

Traceability provides the connective tissue between:

Strategy

Business

Capabilities

Requirements

Architecture

Technology

Data

AI

Agents

Security

Risk

Investment

Implementation

Operations

Outcomes

Value

The fundamental principle is:

AN ARCHITECTURE ELEMENT WITHOUT
TRACEABILITY IS AN ISOLATED FACT.

AN ARCHITECTURE ELEMENT WITH
TRACEABILITY BECOMES PART OF
THE ENTERPRISE ARCHITECTURE SYSTEM.

Traceability therefore enables:

Impact Analysis

Dependency Analysis

Architecture Governance

Decision Support

Risk Analysis

AI Reasoning

Digital Twin Integration

Value Measurement

Continuous Architecture Evolution

The Master Architecture shall treat traceability as a permanent architectural capability rather than as a documentation exercise.

---

# 169. EA-IMETA Master Architecture Maturity and Evolution Framework

## 169.1 Purpose

The EA-IMETA Maturity and Evolution Framework defines how the enterprise architecture capability shall be assessed, developed, measured, and continuously improved.

The purpose is to ensure that EA-IMETA does not become a static architecture framework.

Instead, it shall provide a mechanism through which the enterprise can progress from:

BASIC ARCHITECTURE CAPABILITY

to

INTEGRATED ARCHITECTURE CAPABILITY

to

INTELLIGENT ARCHITECTURE CAPABILITY

to

ADAPTIVE AND AUTONOMOUS ARCHITECTURE

---

# 170. Maturity Principle

## 170.1 Fundamental Principle

EA-IMETA maturity shall be understood as the increasing ability of an enterprise to:

UNDERSTAND

DESIGN

GOVERN

IMPLEMENT

MEASURE

LEARN

ADAPT

AUTOMATE

Architecture maturity is therefore not measured only by the number of architecture documents produced.

A mature architecture capability demonstrates that architecture is:

CONNECTED TO STRATEGY

CONNECTED TO DECISION-MAKING

CONNECTED TO IMPLEMENTATION

CONNECTED TO OPERATIONS

CONNECTED TO VALUE

AND CAPABLE OF EVOLUTION

---

# 171. Maturity Dimensions

EA-IMETA maturity shall be assessed across multiple dimensions.

The initial dimensions shall include:

Architecture Governance

Architecture Capability

Architecture Knowledge

Architecture Integration

Architecture Traceability

Architecture Data

Architecture Technology

AI Architecture

Agent Architecture

Security Architecture

Risk Management

Architecture Automation

Architecture Intelligence

Digital Twin Capability

Value Management

Transformation Capability

Architecture Evolution

---

# 172. Maturity Levels

EA-IMETA shall use a six-level maturity model.

LEVEL 1
INITIAL

LEVEL 2
DEVELOPING

LEVEL 3
DEFINED

LEVEL 4
INTEGRATED

LEVEL 5
ADAPTIVE

LEVEL 6
INTELLIGENT

---

# 173. Level 1 – Initial

## 173.1 Characteristics

Architecture activity is primarily reactive.

Typical characteristics include:

Limited Architecture Governance

Fragmented Documentation

Informal Decisions

Limited Traceability

Technology-Led Decisions

Limited Strategic Alignment

Manual Processes

Limited Architecture Metrics

---

## 173.2 Primary Risk

The principal risk is:

ARCHITECTURE EXISTS

BUT

THE ENTERPRISE DOES NOT MANAGE IT AS A SYSTEM.

---

# 174. Level 2 – Developing

## 174.1 Characteristics

The enterprise begins establishing formal architecture capabilities.

Typical characteristics include:

Defined Architecture Roles

Basic Architecture Governance

Initial Reference Models

Architecture Documentation

Basic Standards

Architecture Reviews

Initial Capability Mapping

Initial Technology Mapping

---

## 174.2 Primary Objective

The objective is:

FROM INFORMAL ARCHITECTURE

TO

DEFINED ARCHITECTURE PRACTICE

---

# 175. Level 3 – Defined

## 175.1 Characteristics

Architecture becomes a recognized enterprise capability.

Characteristics include:

Master Architecture

Domain Architectures

Canonical Vocabulary

Architecture Principles

Architecture Standards

Architecture Decisions

Architecture Repository

Architecture Governance

Traceability

Architecture Metrics

---

## 175.2 Primary Objective

The objective is:

CREATE A CONSISTENT ENTERPRISE ARCHITECTURE SYSTEM

---

# 176. Level 4 – Integrated

## 176.1 Characteristics

Architecture becomes integrated with enterprise management.

Architecture is connected to:

Strategy

Investment

Risk

Security

Transformation

Operations

Performance

Value

---

## 176.2 Primary Objective

The objective is:

ARCHITECTURE BECOMES
A CORE ENTERPRISE MANAGEMENT CAPABILITY

---

# 177. Level 5 – Adaptive

## 177.1 Characteristics

Architecture becomes capable of responding dynamically to change.

Characteristics include:

Continuous Architecture

Automated Discovery

Architecture Drift Detection

Dynamic Risk Assessment

Scenario Analysis

Digital Twin

Architecture Intelligence

AI-Assisted Analysis

Continuous Governance

---

## 177.2 Primary Objective

The objective is:

MOVE FROM STATIC ARCHITECTURE

TO

CONTINUOUSLY ADAPTING ARCHITECTURE

---

# 178. Level 6 – Intelligent

## 178.1 Characteristics

Architecture becomes an intelligent, continuously learning enterprise capability.

Characteristics include:

Architecture AI

Knowledge Graph

Digital Twin

Predictive Architecture

AI-Assisted Decision Support

Autonomous Architecture Analysis

Automated Impact Analysis

Dynamic Governance

Continuous Optimization

Agent-Based Architecture Operations

---

## 178.2 Primary Objective

The objective is:

ARCHITECTURE BECOMES
A SELF-LEARNING AND
INTELLIGENCE-ENABLED ENTERPRISE CAPABILITY

---

# 179. Maturity Progression

The overall maturity progression shall be:

LEVEL 1
INITIAL
   |
   v
LEVEL 2
DEVELOPING
   |
   v
LEVEL 3
DEFINED
   |
   v
LEVEL 4
INTEGRATED
   |
   v
LEVEL 5
ADAPTIVE
   |
   v
LEVEL 6
INTELLIGENT

The progression is not necessarily linear.

An enterprise may have different maturity levels across different architecture domains.

---

# 180. Domain-Specific Maturity

Each architecture domain shall be capable of having its own maturity level.

For example:

Business Architecture       Level 4

Data Architecture           Level 4

Technology Architecture     Level 5

AI Architecture             Level 3

Agent Architecture          Level 2

Security Architecture       Level 5

Digital Twin                Level 1

The overall enterprise architecture maturity shall therefore be based on a balanced assessment rather than a simple average.

---

# 181. Maturity Assessment Model

## 181.1 Assessment Structure

Each maturity assessment shall consider:

Capability

Process

People

Governance

Technology

Data

Evidence

Performance

Integration

Automation

---

## 181.2 Assessment Questions

The assessment should determine:

Does the capability exist?

Is it documented?

Is it governed?

Is it consistently used?

Is it integrated?

Is it measured?

Is it automated?

Is it adaptive?

Is it intelligent?

---

# 182. Architecture Capability Maturity

Architecture capability shall be assessed across:

Architecture Roles

Architecture Skills

Architecture Methods

Architecture Tools

Architecture Governance

Architecture Repository

Architecture Models

Architecture Standards

Architecture Metrics

Architecture Automation

---

# 183. Architecture Knowledge Maturity

Knowledge maturity shall progress through:

DOCUMENTED

↓

STRUCTURED

↓

CONNECTED

↓

MACHINE-READABLE

↓

INTELLIGENT

↓

SELF-IMPROVING

---

# 184. Architecture Data Maturity

Architecture data maturity shall progress through:

MANUAL DATA

↓

CENTRALIZED DATA

↓

INTEGRATED DATA

↓

CONNECTED ARCHITECTURE DATA

↓

REAL-TIME ARCHITECTURE DATA

↓

PREDICTIVE ARCHITECTURE DATA

---

# 185. Architecture Intelligence Maturity

Architecture Intelligence shall progress through:

REPORTING

↓

ANALYSIS

↓

DECISION SUPPORT

↓

PREDICTION

↓

PRESCRIPTION

↓

ADAPTIVE INTELLIGENCE

---

# 186. Digital Twin Maturity

Digital Twin maturity shall progress through:

STATIC MODEL

↓

STRUCTURED MODEL

↓

CONNECTED MODEL

↓

DYNAMIC MODEL

↓

SIMULATED MODEL

↓

PREDICTIVE DIGITAL TWIN

---

# 187. AI Architecture Maturity

AI Architecture maturity shall progress through:

AI EXPERIMENTATION

↓

AI USE CASES

↓

AI SERVICES

↓

ENTERPRISE AI PLATFORM

↓

INTEGRATED AI ARCHITECTURE

↓

INTELLIGENT ENTERPRISE

---

# 188. Agent Architecture Maturity

Agent maturity shall progress through:

ASSISTIVE AGENTS

↓

RECOMMENDATION AGENTS

↓

SUPERVISED AGENTS

↓

CONDITIONAL AUTONOMY

↓

MULTI-AGENT SYSTEMS

↓

GOVERNED AUTONOMOUS ENTERPRISE

---

# 189. Architecture Evolution Model

Architecture evolution shall operate as a continuous cycle.

OBSERVE

↓

UNDERSTAND

↓

ASSESS

↓

IDENTIFY CHANGE

↓

DESIGN

↓

DECIDE

↓

IMPLEMENT

↓

MEASURE

↓

LEARN

↓

EVOLVE

---

# 190. Architecture Observation

The enterprise shall continuously observe:

Business Performance

Technology Performance

Security Events

Risk

Customer Behavior

Operational Events

Cost

Architecture Drift

Market Change

Regulatory Change

Technology Change

AI Development

---

# 191. Architecture Assessment

Observed information shall be assessed against:

Strategy

Architecture Principles

Target Architecture

Risk Appetite

Performance Requirements

Value Objectives

Security Requirements

Regulatory Requirements

---

# 192. Change Identification

Architecture change may be triggered when:

Performance Falls

Risk Increases

Technology Becomes Obsolete

Strategy Changes

Customer Needs Change

Regulation Changes

New Technology Emerges

AI Capabilities Improve

Architecture Debt Becomes Material

---

# 193. Architecture Evolution Decision

The enterprise may respond through:

Maintain

Optimize

Modernize

Replace

Retire

Consolidate

Automate

Augment

Transform

Re-Architect

---

# 194. Architecture Roadmap

The Master Architecture shall support architecture roadmaps.

A roadmap shall connect:

CURRENT STATE

↓

TRANSITION STATES

↓

TARGET STATE

---

## 194.1 Roadmap Dimensions

Roadmaps may cover:

Capabilities

Processes

Applications

Data

Technology

AI

Agents

Security

Organization

Investment

Skills

Operations

---

# 195. Architecture Scenario Planning

Scenario planning shall be used to evaluate possible futures.

Scenarios may include:

Market Growth

Market Decline

Technology Disruption

AI Acceleration

Cybersecurity Crisis

Regulatory Change

Supply Chain Disruption

Economic Pressure

Organizational Transformation

---

# 196. Architecture Simulation

Simulation shall allow architecture alternatives to be evaluated before implementation.

Conceptually:

CURRENT STATE
      |
      v
SCENARIO
      |
      v
ARCHITECTURE OPTION
      |
      v
SIMULATION
      |
      v
EXPECTED OUTCOME
      |
      v
DECISION

---

# 197. Architecture Optimization

Optimization shall seek the best balance between:

Value

Cost

Risk

Performance

Complexity

Resilience

Security

Flexibility

Sustainability

---

# 198. Architecture Portfolio Evolution

The enterprise architecture portfolio shall be continuously evaluated.

Portfolio categories may include:

Invest

Maintain

Modernize

Consolidate

Migrate

Replace

Retire

---

# 199. Architecture Lifecycle Economics

Architecture decisions shall consider lifecycle economics.

The assessment shall include:

Initial Cost

Implementation Cost

Operating Cost

Maintenance Cost

Integration Cost

Security Cost

Change Cost

Technical Debt

Retirement Cost

Residual Value

---

# 200. Architecture Debt Management

Architecture Debt shall be actively managed.

The enterprise shall:

IDENTIFY

↓

CLASSIFY

↓

MEASURE

↓

PRIORITIZE

↓

FUND

↓

REDUCE

↓

MONITOR

---

# 201. Architecture Fitness Monitoring

Architecture Fitness shall be monitored continuously.

Key indicators may include:

Strategic Alignment

Business Performance

Technology Health

Security Health

Data Quality

AI Performance

Agent Performance

Operational Resilience

Cost Efficiency

Architecture Complexity

Technical Debt

---

# 202. Continuous Architecture Governance

At higher maturity levels, governance shall become continuous.

Instead of:

PERIODIC ARCHITECTURE REVIEW

the enterprise shall increasingly operate:

CONTINUOUS ARCHITECTURE OBSERVATION

+

CONTINUOUS RISK ASSESSMENT

+

CONTINUOUS CONFORMANCE

+

CONTINUOUS LEARNING

---

# 203. Architecture Intelligence Feedback

Architecture Intelligence shall feed the Master Architecture.

OBSERVED DATA

↓

ANALYSIS

↓

INSIGHT

↓

ARCHITECTURE KNOWLEDGE

↓

DECISION

↓

CHANGE

↓

NEW OBSERVATION

This creates a closed architecture learning loop.

---

# 204. Self-Learning Architecture

At the highest maturity level, the architecture capability should be able to identify:

Emerging Risks

Emerging Opportunities

Architecture Drift

Performance Patterns

Technology Trends

Capability Gaps

Optimization Opportunities

Potential Transformation Requirements

AI may assist in identifying these patterns.

Human accountability shall remain defined for material decisions.

---

# 205. Architecture Autonomy

Architecture autonomy shall be introduced gradually.

LEVEL 0
Manual

LEVEL 1
Assisted

LEVEL 2
Recommended

LEVEL 3
Supervised Automation

LEVEL 4
Conditional Automation

LEVEL 5
Governed Autonomous Architecture

---

# 206. Autonomous Architecture Principle

The objective is not unlimited automation.

The principle shall be:

AUTOMATE WHERE SAFE

ASSIST WHERE USEFUL

ESCALATE WHERE NECESSARY

RETAIN ACCOUNTABILITY ALWAYS

---

# 207. Master Architecture Evolution

The Master Architecture itself shall evolve.

Changes may be triggered by:

New Architecture Domains

New Technology

AI Development

New Governance Requirements

New Enterprise Models

New Regulatory Requirements

Lessons Learned

Architecture Research

Operational Experience

---

# 208. Master Architecture Versioning

The Master Architecture shall use controlled versioning.

MAJOR

MINOR

PATCH

Major versions shall be used when fundamental architecture structures change.

Minor versions shall be used for substantial additions that preserve the core structure.

Patch versions shall be used for corrections and clarifications.

---

# 209. Master Architecture Review Cycle

The Master Architecture shall be periodically reviewed.

The review shall assess:

Relevance

Completeness

Consistency

Technology Alignment

Business Alignment

Governance Alignment

AI Alignment

Security Alignment

Value

Usability

Future Readiness

---

# 210. Architecture Research and Foresight

EA-IMETA shall maintain an architecture foresight capability.

Foresight shall monitor:

Emerging Technology

AI

Quantum Computing

Automation

Cybersecurity

Regulation

Digital Ecosystems

Economic Change

Environmental Change

Organizational Models

---

# 211. Architecture Innovation

Innovation shall be managed as part of architecture evolution.

The innovation lifecycle shall be:

IDEA

↓

EXPERIMENT

↓

EVALUATE

↓

VALIDATE

↓

ARCHITECT

↓

SCALE

↓

OPERATE

↓

MEASURE

---

# 212. Architecture Learning System

Lessons learned shall be captured from:

Projects

Programs

Operations

Incidents

Architecture Reviews

Architecture Decisions

Experiments

Simulations

AI Systems

Agent Systems

---

# 213. Architecture Knowledge Feedback

Lessons learned shall feed back into:

Principles

Standards

Patterns

Reference Architectures

Methods

Governance

Training

Master Architecture

---

# 214. EA-IMETA Continuous Improvement Cycle

The complete improvement cycle shall be:

PLAN

↓

ARCHITECT

↓

IMPLEMENT

↓

OPERATE

↓

OBSERVE

↓

MEASURE

↓

LEARN

↓

IMPROVE

↓

RE-ARCHITECT

---

# 215. Master Architecture Target Maturity

The long-term target for EA-IMETA shall be:

AN INTEGRATED, INTELLIGENT,
ADAPTIVE AND GOVERNED
ENTERPRISE ARCHITECTURE CAPABILITY

This does not mean that every enterprise must immediately reach Level 6.

The maturity model exists to provide a controlled progression path.

---

# 216. EA-IMETA Maturity Scorecard

A future implementation may use the following scorecard:

| Dimension | Level 1 | Level 2 | Level 3 | Level 4 | Level 5 | Level 6 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Governance | Initial | Developing | Defined | Integrated | Adaptive | Intelligent |
| Architecture | Initial | Developing | Defined | Integrated | Adaptive | Intelligent |
| Traceability | Limited | Basic | Structured | Integrated | Automated | Intelligent |
| Data | Manual | Centralized | Integrated | Connected | Dynamic | Predictive |
| AI | Experimental | Emerging | Defined | Integrated | Adaptive | Intelligent |
| Agents | None | Assistive | Supervised | Conditional | Autonomous | Governed Autonomous |
| Digital Twin | None | Static | Structured | Connected | Dynamic | Predictive |
| Automation | Manual | Assisted | Controlled | Integrated | Adaptive | Autonomous |
| Value | Unclear | Measured | Managed | Integrated | Optimized | Continuously Optimized |

---

# 217. Master Architecture Success Measures

The Master Architecture shall ultimately be evaluated through measurable outcomes.

Success indicators may include:

Improved Strategic Alignment

Reduced Architecture Risk

Reduced Architecture Debt

Improved Decision Quality

Improved Traceability

Improved Reuse

Reduced Complexity

Improved Security

Improved Resilience

Improved Value Realization

Faster Transformation

Improved Architecture Agility

The first Part of the Master Architecture can now be summarized as:

                         EA-IMETA MASTER
                                |

---

# 219. Part 1 Consolidated Principles

Part 1 establishes the following fundamental EA-IMETA principles:

Principle 1
EA-IMETA SHALL BE A CONNECTED ARCHITECTURE SYSTEM.

Principle 2
THE MASTER ARCHITECTURE SHALL GOVERN THE ARCHITECTURE ECOSYSTEM.

Principle 3
THE META-MODEL SHALL PROVIDE THE COMMON STRUCTURAL LANGUAGE.

Principle 4
THE CANONICAL VOCABULARY SHALL PROVIDE THE COMMON SEMANTIC LANGUAGE.

Principle 5
THE REFERENCE LIBRARY SHALL PROVIDE THE KNOWLEDGE FOUNDATION.

Principle 6
GOVERNANCE SHALL PROVIDE AUTHORITY AND ACCOUNTABILITY.

Principle 7
TRACEABILITY SHALL CONNECT ARCHITECTURE ELEMENTS.

Principle 8
ARCHITECTURE SHALL BE MEASURABLE.

Principle 9
ARCHITECTURE SHALL CONTINUOUSLY EVOLVE.

Principle 10
AUTOMATION AND AI SHALL EXTEND ARCHITECTURE CAPABILITY
WITHOUT REMOVING ACCOUNTABILITY.

---

Part 1 of EA-IMETA-MASTER-01 has established the foundational architecture of the EA-IMETA Master Architecture.

It has defined:

ARCHITECTURE SYSTEM.

# 221. EA-IMETA Enterprise Architecture Domain Framework

## 221.1 Purpose

Part 2 of the EA-IMETA Master Architecture establishes the Enterprise Architecture Domain Framework.

Where Part 1 established the foundational architecture of EA-IMETA, Part 2 defines how the enterprise itself shall be represented through a coherent set of architecture domains.

The Domain Framework provides the structural bridge between:

MASTER ARCHITECTURE

and

SPECIALIST ARCHITECTURE

It establishes the principal architectural perspectives required to understand, design, govern, transform, operate, and evolve an enterprise.

---

# 222. Domain Framework Principle

## 222.1 Fundamental Principle

The EA-IMETA Domain Framework shall follow:

THE ENTERPRISE SHALL BE VIEWED
THROUGH MULTIPLE ARCHITECTURAL PERSPECTIVES

BUT

ALL PERSPECTIVES SHALL REMAIN
CONNECTED THROUGH A COMMON
META-MODEL, VOCABULARY,
TRACEABILITY MODEL AND
GOVERNANCE SYSTEM.

---

## 222.2 Domain Independence and Integration

Each architecture domain shall be sufficiently independent to maintain specialist expertise.

At the same time, no domain shall be considered completely isolated.

The fundamental relationship shall therefore be:

DOMAIN SPECIALIZATION

+

CROSS-DOMAIN INTEGRATION

=

ENTERPRISE ARCHITECTURE

---

# 223. Enterprise Architecture Domain Model

The EA-IMETA enterprise shall initially be represented through the following principal domains:

01 Enterprise Strategy Architecture

02 Business Architecture

03 Operating Model Architecture

04 Capability Architecture

05 Value Stream and Process Architecture

06 Organization and People Architecture

07 Information Architecture

08 Data Architecture

09 Application Architecture

10 Technology Architecture

11 Cloud and Platform Architecture

12 AI Architecture

13 Agent Architecture

14 Security Architecture

15 Governance Architecture

16 Risk and Compliance Architecture

17 Resilience Architecture

18 Engineering Architecture

19 Operations Architecture

20 Architecture Intelligence

21 Digital Twin and Simulation

22 Value and Economics

23 Investment and Portfolio Architecture

24 Sustainability Architecture

25 Autonomous Enterprise Architecture

These domains form the initial EA-IMETA Domain Taxonomy.

---

# 224. Domain Classification

Each architecture domain shall have a defined position within the overall architecture.

The domains shall therefore be grouped into architectural families.

STRATEGIC DOMAINS
|
+-- Enterprise Strategy
+-- Value and Economics
+-- Investment and Portfolio
|
BUSINESS DOMAINS
|
+-- Business Architecture
+-- Operating Model
+-- Capability Architecture
+-- Value Streams and Processes
+-- Organization and People
|
INFORMATION DOMAINS
|
+-- Information Architecture
+-- Data Architecture
|
DIGITAL AND TECHNOLOGY DOMAINS
|
+-- Application Architecture
+-- Technology Architecture
+-- Cloud and Platform Architecture
|
INTELLIGENCE DOMAINS
|
+-- AI Architecture
+-- Agent Architecture
+-- Architecture Intelligence
+-- Digital Twin and Simulation
|
CONTROL DOMAINS
|
+-- Security Architecture
+-- Governance Architecture
+-- Risk and Compliance
+-- Resilience Architecture
|
DELIVERY AND OPERATIONS DOMAINS
|
+-- Engineering Architecture
+-- Operations Architecture
|
FUTURE ENTERPRISE DOMAINS
|
+-- Sustainability Architecture
+-- Autonomous Enterprise Architecture

---

# 225. Strategic Architecture Layer

## 225.1 Purpose

The Strategic Architecture Layer establishes why the enterprise exists, where it intends to go, what outcomes it seeks, and how resources shall be directed.

The layer contains:

Enterprise Purpose

Strategic Direction

Strategic Objectives

Strategic Outcomes

Strategic Capabilities

Strategic Initiatives

Investment Priorities

---

## 225.2 Strategic Architecture Relationship

PURPOSE
   |
   v
VISION
   |
   v
STRATEGY
   |
   v
OBJECTIVES
   |
   v
OUTCOMES
   |
   v
CAPABILITIES
   |
   v
TRANSFORMATION

---

# 226. Enterprise Strategy Architecture

## 226.1 Definition

Enterprise Strategy Architecture describes the relationship between enterprise purpose, strategic intent, strategic objectives, capabilities, outcomes, investments, and transformation.

It answers:

WHY DOES THE ENTERPRISE EXIST?

WHERE IS IT GOING?

WHAT MUST IT ACHIEVE?

WHAT CAPABILITIES ARE REQUIRED?

---

## 226.2 Strategic Architecture Components

The domain shall include:

Purpose

Vision

Mission

Strategic Themes

Strategic Objectives

Strategic Outcomes

Strategic Capabilities

Strategic Risks

Strategic Initiatives

Strategic Measures

---

# 227. Business Architecture

## 227.1 Definition

Business Architecture describes the fundamental structure of the business and how it creates and delivers value.

It provides the bridge between strategy and execution.

STRATEGY
   |
   v
BUSINESS ARCHITECTURE
   |
   v
CAPABILITIES
   |
   v
OPERATING MODEL
   |
   v
EXECUTION

---

## 227.2 Business Architecture Components

The domain shall include:

Business Model

Business Capabilities

Value Streams

Business Services

Business Processes

Business Functions

Business Information

Business Relationships

Business Rules

Business Outcomes

---

# 228. Operating Model Architecture

## 228.1 Definition

Operating Model Architecture describes how the enterprise organizes itself to execute strategy and deliver value.

It connects:

PEOPLE

PROCESS

TECHNOLOGY

GOVERNANCE

DECISION-MAKING

RESOURCES

---

## 228.2 Operating Model Components

The domain shall include:

Organizational Structure

Decision Model

Process Model

Service Model

Sourcing Model

Technology Model

Governance Model

Performance Model

---

# 229. Capability Architecture

## 229.1 Definition

Capability Architecture describes what the enterprise must be capable of doing to achieve its objectives.

Capability Architecture is a central integration point within EA-IMETA.

---

## 229.2 Capability Principle

A capability shall remain conceptually independent of the specific implementation used to realize it.

Therefore:

CAPABILITY

IS NOT

PROCESS

IS NOT

APPLICATION

IS NOT

TECHNOLOGY

Instead:

CAPABILITY
   |
   +-- realized through
          |
          +-- Processes
          +-- People
          +-- Applications
          +-- Data
          +-- Technology
          +-- AI
          +-- Agents

---

# 230. Capability Hierarchy

Capabilities may be represented through multiple levels.

ENTERPRISE CAPABILITY
        |
        +-- DOMAIN CAPABILITY
                |
                +-- BUSINESS CAPABILITY
                        |
                        +-- SUB-CAPABILITY

The appropriate depth shall depend on the purpose of the architecture.

---

# 231. Value Stream and Process Architecture

## 231.1 Definition

Value Stream and Process Architecture describes how activities are organized to transform inputs into value-producing outcomes.

The domain shall distinguish between:

VALUE STREAM

PROCESS

ACTIVITY

TASK

These concepts shall not be treated as interchangeable.

---

## 231.2 Value Stream Model

STAKEHOLDER NEED
       |
       v
VALUE STREAM
       |
       v
VALUE STAGES
       |
       v
BUSINESS ACTIVITIES
       |
       v
OUTCOME
       |
       v
VALUE

---

# 232. Organization and People Architecture

## 232.1 Definition

Organization and People Architecture describes the human and organizational structures required to operate the enterprise.

It includes:

Organizational Units

Roles

Responsibilities

Skills

Competencies

Decision Rights

Leadership

Workforce Models

Culture

Collaboration

---

## 232.2 Human Capability

Human capability shall be treated as an architectural asset.

The architecture shall therefore consider:

SKILLS

KNOWLEDGE

EXPERIENCE

CAPACITY

AUTHORITY

ACCOUNTABILITY

LEARNING

---

# 233. Information Architecture

## 233.1 Definition

Information Architecture describes how information is structured, classified, governed, exchanged, interpreted, and used throughout the enterprise.

It provides the semantic bridge between business meaning and data implementation.

---

## 233.2 Information Architecture Components

The domain shall include:

Information Concepts

Information Objects

Information Relationships

Information Flows

Information Classification

Information Ownership

Information Lifecycle

Information Usage

---

# 234. Data Architecture

## 234.1 Definition

Data Architecture describes the structure, ownership, movement, storage, quality, governance, and lifecycle of enterprise data.

It shall address:

Data Domains

Data Entities

Data Products

Data Stores

Data Flows

Data Integration

Data Quality

Data Governance

Data Lineage

Data Lifecycle

## 235.1 Definition

# 236. Technology Architecture

Technology shall be treated as an enabling architecture capability rather than as the primary definition of enterprise architecture.

# 237. Cloud and Platform Architecture

## 237.1 Definition

Cloud and Platform Architecture describes reusable platforms and cloud capabilities that provide scalable technical foundations for enterprise services.

It shall include:

# 239. Agent Architecture

## 239.1 Definition

Agent Architecture describes autonomous and semi-autonomous software entities and the structures through which they perceive, reason, decide, communicate, use tools, and perform authorized actions.

The domain shall include:

Agent Tools

Agent Communication

Agent Policies

# 240. Security Architecture

## 240.1 Definition

Security Architecture describes the structures, controls, principles, and capabilities used to protect enterprise assets, systems, data, people, and operations.

# 241. Governance Architecture

Controls

Risk and Compliance Architecture describes how enterprise risks, regulatory requirements, compliance obligations, controls, and assurance activities are structured and managed.

No architecture domain shall operate independently of the enterprise architecture ecosystem.

   v

   v                  v
PROCESS            ORGANIZATION

        INFORMATION
            |
            v

            |
            v
       APPLICATIONS
            |
            v
        TECHNOLOGY
            |
            v
       CLOUD / PLATFORM

SECURITY

GOVERNANCE

RISK

RESILIENCE

AI

AGENTS

ARCHITECTURE INTELLIGENCE

VALUE

OPERATIONS

# 253. Domain Relationship Principle

Each domain shall be connected through explicit relationships.

Examples include:

Strategy drives Capability

Capability enables Value Stream

Value Stream uses Process

Process requires Organization

Process consumes Information

Information is represented through Data

Capability is supported by Application

Application runs on Technology

Technology is governed by Security

Architecture is governed by Governance

Investment enables Transformation

Operations provides Evidence

Evidence supports Architecture Intelligence

---

# 254. Cross-Domain Architecture

Cross-domain architecture shall address concerns that cannot be fully contained within one domain.

Examples include:

Security

Identity

Risk

AI

Data

Integration

Resilience

Observability

Governance

Value

Each domain shall have an accountable owner.

The ownership model shall contain:

Domain Architect

Domain Steward

Domain Standards

Domain Models

Domain Repository

Domain Metrics

Domain Roadmap

↓

↓

↓

OPERATE

↓

↓

EVOLVE

# 262. Section 1 Summary

EA-IMETA-MASTER-01 Part 2 Section 1 establishes the Enterprise Architecture Domain Framework.

The Domain Framework provides the principal structural organization of enterprise architecture.

It establishes the relationship between:

Strategy

Business

Capabilities

Processes

Organization

Information

Data

Applications

Technology

Cloud

AI

Agents

Security

Governance

Risk

Resilience

Engineering

Operations

Architecture Intelligence

Digital Twin

Value

Investment

Sustainability

Autonomous Enterprise

The central principle is:

NO DOMAIN IS THE ENTERPRISE.

THE ENTERPRISE EXISTS IN THE
RELATIONSHIPS BETWEEN DOMAINS.

The Domain Framework therefore transforms the EA-IMETA architecture from a collection of specialist perspectives into an integrated enterprise architecture system.

---

# 263. EA-IMETA Cross-Domain Integration Framework

## 263.1 Purpose

The EA-IMETA Cross-Domain Integration Framework defines how the individual architecture domains established in Section 1 shall operate as one integrated enterprise architecture.

The purpose is to prevent the architecture domains from becoming isolated specialist silos.

The framework establishes:

- Cross-domain relationships
- Integration principles
- Shared architecture objects
- Dependency management
- Cross-domain decision-making
- Integration governance
- Cross-domain traceability
- Architecture consistency
- Impact analysis

The central objective is:

MULTIPLE DOMAINS

BUT

ONE ENTERPRISE ARCHITECTURE.

---

# 264. Cross-Domain Integration Principle

## 264.1 Fundamental Principle

EA-IMETA shall treat integration between domains as a first-class architecture capability.

The architecture shall therefore not be represented as:

DOMAIN 1
DOMAIN 2
DOMAIN 3
DOMAIN 4

but as:

DOMAIN 1
   |
   +---- DOMAIN 2
   |       |
   |       +---- DOMAIN 3
   |               |
   +---------------+
           |
           v
      ENTERPRISE

---

## 264.2 Integration Over Isolation

Domain specialization is necessary.

Domain isolation is not.

Each domain shall therefore:

- Maintain its specialist integrity
- Use the common EA-IMETA vocabulary
- Use the common meta-model
- Maintain explicit relationships
- Participate in cross-domain governance
- Support traceability
- Expose relevant dependencies

---

# 265. Enterprise Architecture Integration Layers

Cross-domain integration shall operate through several layers.

## 265.1 Semantic Integration

Ensures that domains use consistent meanings.

Examples:

Capability

Service

Data

Application

Risk

Control

Outcome

---

## 265.2 Structural Integration

Ensures that architecture objects are structurally connected.

Examples:

Capability → Application

Application → Technology

Data → Application

Risk → Control

Strategy → Capability

---

## 265.3 Process Integration

Ensures that architecture processes work together.

Examples:

Strategy Planning

Architecture Planning

Investment Planning

Transformation Planning

Implementation

Operations

---

## 265.4 Governance Integration

Ensures that decisions made in one domain remain consistent with decisions in other domains.

---

## 265.5 Operational Integration

Ensures that architecture models reflect operational reality.

---

# 266. Cross-Domain Integration Model

The canonical integration model shall be:

STRATEGY
    |
    v
OBJECTIVES
    |
    v
CAPABILITIES
    |
    +-------------------+
    |                   |
    v                   v
VALUE STREAMS       ORGANIZATION
    |                   |
    v                   |
PROCESSES <-------------+
    |
    v
INFORMATION
    |
    v
DATA
    |
    v
APPLICATIONS
    |
    v
TECHNOLOGY
    |
    v
PLATFORMS
    |
    +------------------+
    |                  |
    v                  v
     AI              AGENTS
    |                  |
    +--------+---------+
             |
             v
          ACTION
             |
             v
         OPERATIONS
             |
             v
           VALUE

Cross-cutting controls:

SECURITY
GOVERNANCE
RISK
COMPLIANCE
RESILIENCE

shall operate across the complete model.

---

# 267. Shared Architecture Objects

Cross-domain integration shall be based on shared architecture objects.

The primary shared objects shall include:

Enterprise

Organization

Stakeholder

Purpose

Strategy

Objective

Outcome

Capability

Value Stream

Process

Service

Information

Data

Application

Technology Component

Platform

AI Model

AI Service

Agent

Risk

Threat

Control

Requirement

Architecture Decision

Architecture Principle

Investment

Initiative

Project

Metric

---

# 268. Shared Object Ownership

An architecture object may have:

One Primary Owner

Multiple Consumers

Multiple Relationships

Multiple Domain Contexts

For example:

A Capability may be owned within Business Architecture but consumed by:

Application Architecture

Data Architecture

Technology Architecture

Security Architecture

Investment Architecture

Operations Architecture

---

# 269. Canonical Object Principle

A shared object shall have one canonical identity.

Different domains shall not independently create duplicate representations of the same enterprise object unless there is a documented reason.

For example:

ONE CAPABILITY

rather than:

Business Capability

Technology Capability

Application Capability

Data Capability

when all represent the same underlying enterprise capability.

Domain-specific views may exist, but the underlying identity shall remain traceable.

---

# 270. Cross-Domain Relationship Types

The following relationship types shall be preferred:

drives

supports

enables

requires

realizes

implements

uses

depends-on

contains

owns

governs

constrains

protects

mitigates

funds

measures

produces

consumes

transforms

replaces

supersedes

---

# 271. Relationship Semantics

## 271.1 Drives

Indicates that one architecture object provides direction or causes another object to be established.

Example:

Strategy drives Capability.

---

## 271.2 Supports

Indicates that one object contributes to another without necessarily being essential.

Example:

Application supports Capability.

---

## 271.3 Enables

Indicates that one object makes another object possible.

Example:

Technology enables Application Service.

---

## 271.4 Requires

Indicates a dependency or necessary condition.

Example:

Process requires Data.

---

## 271.5 Realizes

Indicates that an implementation provides a defined capability or requirement.

Example:

Application realizes Capability.

---

## 271.6 Implements

Indicates that an implementation realizes an architecture decision, standard, pattern, or requirement.

---

## 271.7 Depends-On

Indicates a dependency that may create impact if the source or target changes.

---

## 271.8 Governs

Indicates that a policy, authority, or governance mechanism controls another object.

---

## 271.9 Protects

Indicates that a security or resilience control protects an asset or capability.

---

## 271.10 Mitigates

Indicates that a control or action reduces an identified risk.

---

# 272. Cross-Domain Dependency Model

Each major architecture domain shall identify dependencies.

Example:

Business Architecture
    |
    +-- depends on --> Organization
    |
    +-- depends on --> Information
    |
    +-- depends on --> Data
    |
    +-- depends on --> Applications
    |
    +-- depends on --> Technology

Data Architecture
    |
    +-- depends on --> Business Meaning
    |
    +-- depends on --> Applications
    |
    +-- depends on --> Technology
    |
    +-- depends on --> Security

AI Architecture
    |
    +-- depends on --> Data
    |
    +-- depends on --> Technology
    |
    +-- depends on --> Security
    |
    +-- depends on --> Governance

Agent Architecture
    |
    +-- depends on --> AI
    |
    +-- depends on --> Data
    |
    +-- depends on --> Tools
    |
    +-- depends on --> Identity
    |
    +-- depends on --> Security
    |
    +-- depends on --> Governance

---

# 273. Cross-Domain Dependency Classification

Dependencies shall be classified as:

Mandatory

Strong

Moderate

Weak

Informational

---

## 273.1 Mandatory Dependency

The target is required for the source to function.

---

## 273.2 Strong Dependency

The source can technically operate without the target, but significant degradation or risk would occur.

---

## 273.3 Moderate Dependency

The relationship provides meaningful support but alternative mechanisms exist.

---

## 273.4 Weak Dependency

The relationship is useful but not operationally critical.

---

## 273.5 Informational Dependency

The relationship provides context rather than operational dependency.

---

# 274. Cross-Domain Integration Matrix

A future EA-IMETA implementation should maintain an integration matrix.

Example:

| Domain | Strategy | Business | Data | Application | Technology | Security | AI | Operations |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Strategy | — | High | Medium | Medium | Low | Medium | Medium | Medium |
| Business | High | — | High | High | Medium | High | Medium | High |
| Data | Medium | High | — | High | High | High | High | High |
| Application | Medium | High | High | — | High | High | High | High |
| Technology | Low | Medium | High | High | — | High | High | High |
| Security | High | High | High | High | High | — | High | High |
| AI | Medium | High | High | High | High | High | — | High |
| Operations | Medium | High | High | High | High | High | High | — |

The exact dependency intensity shall be established according to the enterprise context.

---

# 275. Cross-Domain Architecture Views

A single architecture model cannot provide sufficient detail for every stakeholder.

EA-IMETA shall therefore support multiple architecture views over the same underlying architecture objects.

Possible views include:

Strategic View

Business View

Capability View

Process View

Information View

Data View

Application View

Technology View

Security View

AI View

Agent View

Operations View

Investment View

Risk View

Value View

---

# 276. Single Model, Multiple Views

The architecture shall distinguish between:

MODEL

and

VIEW.

The canonical architecture model contains the authoritative objects and relationships.

Views provide specific representations for particular stakeholders.

Therefore:

ONE CANONICAL MODEL

+

MANY PURPOSE-SPECIFIC VIEWS

---

# 277. Architecture View Principle

A view shall never silently redefine the underlying architecture.

If a view requires a different interpretation, the underlying model shall be reviewed.

This prevents visual representations from becoming competing architecture truths.

---

# 278. Cross-Domain Information Flow

Information shall flow between architecture domains.

Example:

STRATEGY
    |
    v
BUSINESS REQUIREMENT
    |
    v
CAPABILITY
    |
    v
PROCESS
    |
    v
INFORMATION REQUIREMENT
    |
    v
DATA
    |
    v
APPLICATION
    |
    v
TECHNOLOGY
    |
    v
OPERATIONAL SERVICE
    |
    v
MEASUREMENT
    |
    v
STRATEGIC FEEDBACK

This creates a closed architecture information loop.

---

# 279. Architecture Feedback

Operational and implementation data shall flow back toward the strategic layers.

The model shall therefore support:

FORWARD FLOW

Strategy
→ Capability
→ Architecture
→ Implementation
→ Operation

and:

BACKWARD FLOW

Operation
→ Measurement
→ Insight
→ Architecture
→ Strategy

---

# 280. Cross-Domain Decision Framework

Cross-domain decisions shall be evaluated according to their total enterprise impact.

A decision shall consider, where applicable:

Business Impact

Capability Impact

Data Impact

Application Impact

Technology Impact

Security Impact

AI Impact

Agent Impact

Operational Impact

Financial Impact

Risk Impact

Regulatory Impact

---

# 281. Cross-Domain Architecture Decision

A cross-domain architecture decision shall contain:

Decision ID

Decision Context

Affected Domains

Problem Statement

Drivers

Options

Cross-Domain Dependencies

Risk

Cost

Value

Decision

Rationale

Consequences

Owner

Approval

Review Date

---

# 282. Cross-Domain Impact Analysis

Before major changes, the architecture should identify:

Directly Affected Objects

Indirectly Affected Objects

Dependent Domains

Affected Capabilities

Affected Processes

Affected Data

Affected Applications

Affected Technology

Affected Security Controls

Affected AI Systems

Affected Agents

Affected Operations

Affected Investments

---

# 283. Impact Propagation

Impact propagation shall be represented as:

CHANGE
   |
   v
ARCHITECTURE OBJECT
   |
   v
DIRECT DEPENDENCIES
   |
   v
INDIRECT DEPENDENCIES
   |
   v
DOMAIN IMPACT
   |
   v
ENTERPRISE IMPACT
   |
   v
VALUE / RISK IMPACT

---

# 284. Cross-Domain Consistency

The architecture shall maintain consistency across:

Definitions

Identifiers

Relationships

Ownership

Lifecycle

Version

Status

Standards

Principles

Policies

Architecture Decisions

---

# 285. Consistency Rules

The following rules shall apply.

## Rule 1

A shared object shall have one canonical identity.

## Rule 2

A shared relationship shall have one defined semantic meaning.

## Rule 3

Conflicting domain definitions shall be escalated.

## Rule 4

Architecture views shall reference the canonical model.

## Rule 5

Material changes shall trigger impact analysis.

## Rule 6

Cross-domain decisions shall be traceable.

## Rule 7

Exceptions shall be explicitly governed.

---

# 286. Cross-Domain Architecture Services

EA-IMETA may provide reusable architecture services across domains.

Examples:

Architecture Repository

Architecture Search

Dependency Analysis

Impact Analysis

Architecture Review

Standards Validation

Risk Analysis

Architecture Conformance

Traceability

Scenario Analysis

Architecture Intelligence

---

# 287. Cross-Domain Governance

Cross-domain governance shall be coordinated through the EA-IMETA governance framework.

The governance structure shall include:

Master Architecture Authority

Domain Architecture Authorities

Cross-Domain Review Groups

Security Authority

Data Authority

AI Authority

Technology Authority

Risk Authority

Investment Authority

Operational Authority

---

# 288. Cross-Domain Escalation

A domain shall escalate a decision when:

Multiple domains are materially affected

Strategic objectives are affected

Enterprise risk is affected

Regulatory obligations are affected

Material investment is affected

Architecture principles are affected

Security boundaries are affected

AI or agent autonomy is affected

---

# 289. Cross-Domain Architecture Council

A future implementation may establish a Cross-Domain Architecture Council.

The council should address:

Major Cross-Domain Decisions

Conflicting Architecture Priorities

Architecture Dependencies

Standards Conflicts

Architecture Risk

Architecture Exceptions

Transformation Dependencies

---

# 290. Cross-Domain Architecture Review

Reviews shall evaluate the complete architecture impact rather than only the originating domain.

For example:

A new AI application is not only an AI Architecture decision.

It may affect:

Business

Capability

Data

Application

Technology

Security

Risk

Governance

Operations

Investment

Value

---

# 291. Architecture Integration with Transformation

Transformation initiatives shall use the cross-domain architecture model.

TRANSFORMATION OBJECTIVE
        |
        v
CAPABILITY CHANGE
        |
        +-------------------------+
        |                         |
        v                         v
PROCESS CHANGE              ORGANIZATION CHANGE
        |
        v
INFORMATION / DATA CHANGE
        |
        v
APPLICATION CHANGE
        |
        v
TECHNOLOGY CHANGE
        |
        +-------------------------+
        |                         |
        v                         v
AI / AGENT CHANGE          SECURITY CHANGE
        |
        v
OPERATIONAL CHANGE
        |
        v
VALUE REALIZATION

---

# 292. Architecture Integration with Portfolio Management

Portfolio decisions shall consider cross-domain dependencies.

For each initiative:

INITIATIVE
   |
   +-- Strategic Alignment
   |
   +-- Capability Impact
   |
   +-- Architecture Impact
   |
   +-- Technology Impact
   |
   +-- Security Impact
   |
   +-- Risk Impact
   |
   +-- Investment Impact
   |
   +-- Value Impact

---

# 293. Architecture Integration with Operations

Operations shall provide evidence to architecture.

Operational data may include:

Availability

Performance

Capacity

Cost

Incidents

Security Events

Usage

Customer Experience

Architecture Drift

Technical Debt

AI Performance

Agent Activity

This evidence shall be used to improve architecture decisions.

---

# 294. Architecture Drift Across Domains

Architecture drift may occur independently within each domain.

Examples:

Business Drift

Data Drift

Application Drift

Technology Drift

Security Drift

AI Drift

Agent Drift

Operational Drift

Cross-domain drift occurs when these changes create inconsistencies between domains.

---

# 295. Cross-Domain Drift Detection

The architecture should identify conditions such as:

Capability with no supporting application

Application with no business purpose

Data with no owner

Technology with no consuming service

AI model with no governed use case

Agent with excessive authority

Security control with no associated risk

Investment with no strategic objective

---

# 296. Cross-Domain Architecture Debt

Architecture debt shall be evaluated across domains.

Examples include:

Duplicate Capabilities

Duplicate Applications

Redundant Data

Unmanaged Integrations

Technology Obsolescence

Uncontrolled AI

Agent Permission Accumulation

Governance Gaps

Missing Traceability

---

# 297. Cross-Domain Optimization

Architecture optimization shall seek enterprise-wide improvement rather than local optimization.

A local improvement shall not be considered successful if it creates a greater enterprise-wide cost or risk.

Therefore:

LOCAL OPTIMIZATION

must be evaluated against:

ENTERPRISE OPTIMIZATION.

---

# 298. Architecture Trade-Offs

Cross-domain decisions frequently involve trade-offs.

Examples:

Cost vs Resilience

Speed vs Governance

Innovation vs Risk

Automation vs Human Oversight

Flexibility vs Standardization

Performance vs Cost

Centralization vs Autonomy

Security vs Usability

---

# 299. Trade-Off Documentation

Material trade-offs shall be recorded in architecture decisions.

The decision record shall identify:

Trade-Off

Options

Benefits

Costs

Risks

Accepted Consequences

Rejected Alternatives

Review Conditions

---

# 300. Cross-Domain Architecture Patterns

The following patterns should be recognized as foundational integration patterns.

## 300.1 Strategy-to-Execution Pattern

STRATEGY
→ OBJECTIVE
→ CAPABILITY
→ INITIATIVE
→ ARCHITECTURE
→ IMPLEMENTATION
→ OUTCOME

---

## 300.2 Capability-to-Technology Pattern

CAPABILITY
→ SERVICE
→ APPLICATION
→ PLATFORM
→ TECHNOLOGY

---

## 300.3 Data-to-Decision Pattern

DATA
→ INFORMATION
→ INSIGHT
→ DECISION
→ ACTION
→ OUTCOME

---

## 300.4 Risk-to-Control Pattern

THREAT
→ RISK
→ CONTROL
→ ASSURANCE
→ EVIDENCE

---

## 300.5 AI-to-Outcome Pattern

BUSINESS NEED
→ AI USE CASE
→ MODEL
→ AI SERVICE
→ DECISION
→ ACTION
→ OUTCOME

---

## 300.6 Agent-to-Action Pattern

OBJECTIVE
→ AGENT
→ AUTHORITY
→ TOOL
→ ACTION
→ VALIDATION
→ OUTCOME

---

# 301. Cross-Domain Integration Maturity

Cross-domain integration shall mature through:

LEVEL 1
Isolated

LEVEL 2
Referenced

LEVEL 3
Connected

LEVEL 4
Integrated

LEVEL 5
Dynamic

LEVEL 6
Intelligent

---

## 301.1 Level 1 – Isolated

Domains operate primarily independently.

---

## 301.2 Level 2 – Referenced

Domains begin referencing each other.

---

## 301.3 Level 3 – Connected

Shared objects and relationships are established.

---

## 301.4 Level 4 – Integrated

Cross-domain processes and governance operate consistently.

---

## 301.5 Level 5 – Dynamic

Architecture relationships are continuously updated from operational evidence.

---

## 301.6 Level 6 – Intelligent

AI and architecture intelligence support cross-domain reasoning, impact analysis, and optimization.

---

Possible metrics include:

Cross-Domain Relationship Coverage

Architecture Drift Rate

Consistency

Currency

Value Alignment

---

The EA-IMETA repository shall support:

# 310. Section 2 Summary

EA-IMETA-MASTER-01 Part 2 Section 2 establishes the Cross-Domain Integration Framework.

The framework ensures that the architecture domains established in Section 1 operate as an integrated system.

The principal principles are:

ONE CANONICAL OBJECT

ONE CANONICAL MEANING

EXPLICIT RELATIONSHIPS

BIDIRECTIONAL TRACEABILITY

CROSS-DOMAIN GOVERNANCE

ENTERPRISE-WIDE IMPACT ANALYSIS

LOCAL OPTIMIZATION MUST NOT
UNDERMINE ENTERPRISE OPTIMIZATION

The Cross-Domain Integration Framework provides the connective structure between:

Strategy

Business

Capabilities

Processes

Organization

Information

Data

Applications

Technology

Cloud

AI

Agents

Security

Governance

Risk

Resilience

Operations

Investment

Value

The resulting architecture is therefore not simply a collection of domains.

It is a:

CONNECTED

TRACEABLE

GOVERNED

DEPENDENCY-AWARE

VALUE-ORIENTED

ENTERPRISE ARCHITECTURE SYSTEM.

---
